\|f-F\|_{\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)}+\|f-F\|_{\Psi^{\prime}\mathbb{H}_{p^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(\Omega,T)}<\epsilon\,.

This yields that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\eta_{1},\,\ldots,\,\eta_{N}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big((0,T)\big)
and
f_{1},\,\ldots,\,f_{N}\in X
such that

\|\,f-\widetilde{f}\,\|_{L_{p}((0,T];X)}<\epsilon\,\,,\,\,\text{where}\,\,\,\,\widetilde{f}(t,\cdot)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\eta_{i}(t)f_{i}(\cdot)\,.

Due to Lemma
3.12
.(5), the proof is completed.
∎

We end this subsection with the following parabolic embedding theorem for the space
\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega)
, which is used in Subsections
5.1
and
6.4
:

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy
1/p<\beta\leq 1
.
Then for any
u\in\Psi\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+2}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)
, and
0\leq s<t\leq T
,

\displaystyle\|u(t)-u(s)\|_{\Psi H_{p,\theta+2p\beta}^{\gamma+2-2\beta}(\Omega)}\leq N|t-s|^{\beta-1/p}\Big(\|u\|_{\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)}+\|\partial_{t}u\|_{\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)}\Big)

where
N=N(d,p,\gamma,\theta,\beta)
.

Proof.

The map
f\mapsto\Psi^{-1}f
is an isometric isomorphism from
\Psi H_{p,\theta^{\prime}}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(\Omega)
(resp.
\Psi B_{p,\theta^{\prime}}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(\Omega)
,
\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta^{\prime}}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(\Omega,T)
) to
H_{p,\theta^{\prime}}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(\Omega)
(resp.
B_{p,\theta^{\prime}}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(\Omega)
,
\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta^{\prime}}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(\Omega,T)
), for all
\gamma^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore we only need to prove this proposition for the case
\Psi\equiv 1
.
The proof of this case is provided in
[
48
]
, and we introduce this proof for reader’s convenience.
Since
u\in\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega,T)
,

u_{n}(t,x):=u(t,e^{n}x)\zeta_{0,(n)}(e^{n}x)\in\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)

satisfies that

\big(\partial_{t}u_{n}\big)(s)=\partial_{t}u(s,e^{n}\,\cdot\,)\zeta_{0,(n)}(e^{n}\,\cdot)\quad;\quad u_{n}(0,\cdot\,)=u_{0}(e^{n}\,\cdot\,)\zeta_{0,(n)}(e^{n}\,\cdot\,)

in the sense of distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Since
u_{n}\in\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
and
\partial_{t}u_{n}\in\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
, by
[
48
, Theorem 7.3]
with
a=e^{-np}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&e^{n(2p\beta)}\|u_{n}(t)-u_{n}(s)\|_{H_{p}^{\gamma+2-2\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\\
\leq N&|t-s|^{\beta p-1}\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\|u_{n}(r,\cdot)\|_{H_{p}^{\gamma+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}+e^{2np}\|\partial_{t}u_{n}(r,\cdot\,)\|_{H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r\end{split}

(4.18)

where
N=N(d,p,\gamma,\beta)
.
In fact,
[
48
, Theorem 7.3]
considers the case that
v\,(=u_{n})\in\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
satisfies
\partial_{t}v\in\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
and
v(0)\in H_{p}^{\gamma+2-2/p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
However, (
4.18
) can be obtained without any additional assumptions on
u_{n}
.
This is because
[
48
, Theorem 7.3]
is a consequence of
[
44
, Theorem 7.2]
, and the proof of
[
44
, Theorem 7.2]
only requires that
v\in\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
and
\partial_{t}v\in\mathbb{H}_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
, without assuming
v(0)\in H_{p}^{\gamma+2-2/p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Consequently we obtain

\displaystyle\|u(t)-u(s)\|_{H_{p,\theta+2p\beta}^{\gamma+2-2\beta}(\Omega)}^{p}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{n(\theta+2p\beta)}\|u_{n}(t)-u_{n}(s)\|_{H_{p}^{\gamma+2-\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{N}

\displaystyle|t-s|^{\beta p-1}\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{n\theta}\Big(\|u_{n}(r,\cdot)\|_{H_{p}^{\gamma+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}+e^{2np}\|\partial_{t}u_{n}(r,\cdot\,)\|_{H_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|t-s|^{\beta p-1}\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\|u(r,\cdot)\|_{H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega)}^{p}+\|\partial_{t}u(r,\cdot)\|_{H_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}^{p}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r\,.

∎

4.3.
Solvability of parabolic equations

In this subsection, assuming (
3.16
) and (
4.1
), we introduce the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let

\Omega
admit the Hardy inequality (
1.3
);

\psi
be a superharmonic Harnack function on
\Omega
;

\mu\in I(\psi,p,\nu_{1}/\nu_{2})
.

and suppose that
\Psi
is a regularization of
\psi
.
Then for any

u_{0}\in\Psi^{\mu}B^{\gamma+2-2/p}_{p,d}(\Omega)\quad\text{and}\quad f\in\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p,d+2p-2}(\Omega,T)\,,

the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\mathcal{L}u+f\quad\text{in}\,\,\,(0,T]\quad;\quad u(0,\cdot)=u_{0}

(4.19)

has a unique solution
u
in
\Psi^{\mu}\,\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+2}_{p,d-2}(\Omega,T)
.
Moreover, for this solution
u
, we have

\|u\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+2}_{p,d-2}(\Omega,T)}\leq N\Big(\|u_{0}\|_{\Psi^{\mu}B^{\gamma}_{p,d}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p,d+2p-2}(\Omega,T)}\Big)\,,

where
N=N(d,p,\gamma,\mu,\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega),\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi),\mathrm{C}_{3}(\psi,\Psi),\mathrm{C}_{4})
.

Recall that
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega)
is the constant in (
1.3
),
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi)
and
\mathrm{C}_{3}(\psi,\Psi)
are the constants in Definition
3.1
, and
\mathrm{C}_{4}
is the constant in Definition
4.1
.

Remark 4.13
.

As mentioned in Remark
3.19
, Theorem
4.12
can be reformulated without including
\Psi
.
In addition, when considering the case
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, an equivalent norm of
\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)
is implied by Lemma
3.8
.
An equivalent norm of
\Psi^{\mu}B_{p,d}^{\gamma+2-2/p}(\Omega)
is also provided by (
4.14
) when
p\neq 2
, and Lemmas
4.5
.(4) and
3.8
when
p=2
.
For equivalent norms in the case where
-\gamma\in\mathbb{N}
, Corollary
3.16
and Remark
4.6
can be used.

The proof of Theorem
4.12
is parallel with the proof of Theorem
3.18
.
We begin with introducing a well known counterpart of (
3.30
).

Lemma 4.14
.

Suppose that
u\in\mathbb{H}^{\gamma+1}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
and
f\in\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
satisfies
u(0,\cdot)\in B^{\gamma+2-2/p}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\mathcal{L}u+f\quad\text{in}\,\,t\in(0,T]

(4.20)

in the sense of distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then
u\in\mathbb{H}^{\gamma+2}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
, and

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathbb{H}^{\gamma+2}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}\leq N\Big(\|u\|_{\mathbb{H}^{\gamma+1}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}+\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}+\|u(0,\cdot)\|_{B^{\gamma+2-2/p}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big)

where
N=N(d,p,\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
.

Proof.

We first consider the case
T<\infty
.
By applying the operator
(1-\Delta)^{\gamma/2}
to both sides of (
4.20
), we only need to prove for
\gamma=0
.
Since
u(0)\in B_{p}^{2-2/p}
,
[
51
, Section 4.3]
yields that there exists
\overline{u}\in\mathbb{H}_{p}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)
such that

\partial_{t}\overline{u}=\Delta\overline{u}\quad\text{;}\quad\overline{u}(0)=u(0)\,,

and

\|\overline{u}_{xx}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}\lesssim_{d,p}\|u_{0}\|_{B_{p}^{2-2/p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

Put
w=u-\overline{u}
so that

\partial_{t}w=\mathcal{L}w+f+(\mathcal{L}-\Delta)\overline{u}\quad;\quad w(0)=0\,.

It is implied by
[
47
, Theorem 1.2]
that

\|w_{xx}\|_{\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}\lesssim_{d,p,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}+\|(\mathcal{L}-\Delta)\overline{u}\|_{\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}.

Therefore we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathbb{H}_{p}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p}

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}+\|u_{xx}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}

(4.21)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}+\|\overline{u}_{xx}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}+\|w_{xx}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}+\|u_{0}\|_{B_{p}^{2-2/p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}+\|f\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},T)}\,.

For
T=\infty
, obtain (
4.21
) with
T
replaced by
K\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
K\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

The next two lemmas are driven along the same lines as the proofs of Lemmas
3.22
and
3.23
, respectively.
We leave the proofs to the reader.
For proving Lemma
4.15
, put
v_{n}(t,x)=\zeta_{0,(n)}(e^{n}x)\Psi^{-1}(e^{n}x)u(e^{2n}t,e^{n}x)
(
cf
. (
3.27
)).

Lemma 4.15

(Higher order estimates)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
u\in\Psi\mathbb{H}^{s+2}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)
and
f\in\Psi\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p,\theta+2p}(\Omega,T)
satisfy
u(0,\,\cdot\,)\in B_{p,\theta+2}^{\gamma+2-2/p}(\Omega)
, and

\partial_{t}u=\mathcal{L}u+f\quad\text{in}\,\,(0,T]

in the sense of distributions.
Then

u
belongs to
\Psi\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)
, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|u\|_{\Psi\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+2}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)}\leq N\Big(\|u\|_{\Psi\mathbb{H}^{s}_{p,\theta}(\Omega,T)}+\|f\|_{\Psi\mathbb{H}^{\gamma}_{p,\theta+2p}(\Omega,T)}+\|u(0)\|_{\Psi B^{\gamma+2-2/p}_{p,\theta+2}(\Omega)}\Big)\end{split}

where
N=N(d,p,\gamma,\theta,\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi),s)
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Assume the following:

For any
u_{0}\in\Psi B_{p,\theta+2}^{\gamma+2-2/p}(\Omega)
and
f\in\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
, (
4.19
) has a unique solution
u
in
\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\|\Psi^{-1}u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{\gamma+2}(\Omega)}\leq N_{\gamma}\Big(\|\Psi^{-1}f\|_{H_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}+\|\Psi^{-1}u_{0}\|_{B_{p,\theta+2}^{\gamma+2-2/p}(\Omega)}\Big)

where
N_{\gamma}
is a constant independent of
u_{0}
,
f
and
u
.

Then for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, the following holds:

For any
u_{0}\in\Psi B_{p,\theta+2}^{s+2-2/p}(\Omega)
and
f\in\Psi\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{s}(\Omega)
, (
4.19
) has a unique solution
u
in
\Psi\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{s+2}(\Omega)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\|\Psi^{-1}u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{p,\theta}^{s+2}(\Omega)}\leq N_{s}\Big(\|\Psi^{-1}f\|_{H_{p,\theta+2p}^{s}(\Omega)}+\|\Psi^{-1}u_{0}\|_{B_{p,\theta+2}^{\gamma+2-2/p}(\Omega)}\Big)

where
N_{s}
is a constant depending only on
d,\,p,\,\gamma,\,\theta,\,\nu_{1},\,\nu_{2},\,\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi),\,N_{\gamma},\,s
.

Proof of Theorem
4.12
.

By Lemma
4.16
, we only need to prove for
\gamma=0
.

A priori
estimates.
Make use of Theorem
4.3
and Lemmas
3.8
and
4.5
.(3) to obtain that for any
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T]\times\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|u\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{L}_{p,d-2}(\Omega,T)}&\lesssim_{N}\|u(0,\cdot)\|_{\Psi^{\mu}L_{p,d}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{L}u\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{L}_{p,d+2p-2}(\Omega,T)}\\
&\lesssim_{N}\|u(0,\cdot)\|_{\Psi^{\mu}B_{p,d}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{L}u\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{L}_{p,d+2p-2}(\Omega,T)}\,.\end{split}

By combining this with Lemma
4.15
, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{2}(\Omega,T)}\lesssim_{N}

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{L}_{p,d-2}(\Omega,T)}

(4.22)

\displaystyle+\|u(0,\cdot)\|_{\Psi^{\mu}B_{p,d}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{L}u\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{L}_{p,d+2p-2}(\Omega,T)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{N}

\displaystyle\|u(0,\cdot)\|_{\Psi^{\mu}B_{p,d}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{L}u\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{L}_{p,d+2p-2}(\Omega,T)}\,,

where
N=N(d,p,\mu,\mathrm{C}_{0},\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi),\mathrm{C}_{3}(\psi,\Psi),\mathrm{C}_{4})
.
By Proposition
4.9
, (
4.22
) also holds for all
u\in\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{2}(\Omega,T)
.
Therefore the
a priori
estimates are obtained.
The uniqueness of solutions also follows from (
4.22
).

Existence of solutions.

We first consider the case
\mathcal{L}=\nu_{1}\Delta
.
Let

(f,u_{0})\in\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{L}_{p,d+2p-2}(\Omega,T)\times\Psi^{\mu}B_{p,d}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)=:\mathcal{F}\,.

By Lemma
4.10
and Lemma
4.5
.(1) (the counterpart of Lemma
3.10
.(1),
C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T]\times\Omega)\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is dense in
\mathcal{F}
.
Therefore there exists
(f^{(n)},u^{(n)}_{0})\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T]\times\Omega)\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that
(f^{(n)},u_{0}^{(n)})\rightarrow(f,u_{0})
in
\mathcal{F}
.
Make use of Lemmas
4.4
and
4.15
to obtain that there exists a solution
u^{(n)}\in\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{2}(\Omega,T)
of the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u^{(n)}=\nu_{1}\Delta u^{(n)}+f_{n}\quad;\quad u^{(n)}(0)=u^{(n)}_{0}\,.

By (
4.22
),
u^{(n)}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{2}(\Omega,T)
.
Since
\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{2}(\Omega,T)
is a Banach space, there exists
u\in\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{2}(\Omega,T)
such that
u^{(n)}\rightarrow u
in
\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{2}(\Omega,T)
.
We also obtain that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\big(f^{(n)},u^{(n)}_{0}\big)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\big(\partial_{t}u^{(n)}-\nu_{1}\Delta u^{(n)},u^{(n)}(0)\big)=\big(\partial_{t}u-\nu_{1}\Delta u,u(0)\big)\quad\text{in}\,\,\,\mathcal{F}\,,

which implies
\partial_{t}u-\nu_{1}\Delta u=f
and
u(0)=u_{0}
.
Therefore, we have proven the theorem for the case
\mathcal{L}=\nu_{1}\Delta
.

Let us consider a general
\mathcal{L}:=\sum_{i,j}a^{ij}D_{ij}\in\mathcal{M}_{T}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
, where
(a^{ij}(t))_{d\times d}\in\mathrm{M}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
for all
t\in(0,T]
.
For
s\in[0,1]
, put

\mathcal{L}_{s}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\big((1-s)\nu_{1}\delta^{ij}+sa^{ij}\big)D_{ij}\,.

Since

\displaystyle\nu_{1}|\xi|^{2}\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\big((1-s)\nu_{1}\delta^{ij}+sa^{ij}(t)\big)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\leq\nu_{2}|\xi|^{2}

(4.23)

for all
t\in(0,T]
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{s}\in\mathcal{M}_{T}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
.
It follows from (
4.22
) that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{2}(\Omega,T)}\leq N\Big(\|u(0,\cdot)\|_{\Psi^{\mu}B_{p,d}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{L}_{s}u\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{L}_{p,d+2p-2}(\Omega,T)}\Big)

for all
u\in\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{2}(\Omega,T)
and
s\in[0,1]
, where
N
is the constant in (
4.22
).
In particular,
N
is independent of
s
.
Since the unique solvability for
\mathcal{L}_{0}
, the method of continuity (see,
e.g.
,
[
22
, Theorem 5.2]
) yields the unique solvability for
\mathcal{L}_{1}
.
∎

We end this subsection with the global uniqueness of solutions.

Theorem 4.17

(Global uniqueness)
.

Suppose that (
1.3
) holds for
\Omega
, and for
k=1,\,2
,

\begin{gathered}\text{$\psi_{k}$ is a superharmonic Harnack functions on $\Omega$}\,\,,\quad\text{$\Psi_{k}$ is a regularization of $\psi_{k}$}\,\,,\\
\gamma_{k}\in\mathbb{R}\,\,,\,\,\,\,p_{k}\in(1,\infty)\quad\text{and}\quad\mu_{k}\in I(\psi_{k},p_{k},\nu_{2}/\nu_{1})\,.\end{gathered}

Let

f\in\bigcap_{k=1,2}\Psi_{k}^{\,\mu_{k}}\mathbb{H}_{p_{k},d+2p_{k}-2}^{\gamma_{k}}(\Omega,T)\quad\text{and}\quad u_{0}\in\bigcap_{k=1,2}B_{p_{k},d}^{\gamma_{k}+2-2/p_{k}}(\Omega),

and for each
k=1,\,2
,

u^{(k)}\in\Psi_{k}^{\,\mu_{k}}\mathcal{H}_{p_{k},d-2}^{\gamma_{k}+2}(\Omega,T)
be the solution to the equation

\partial_{t}u^{(k)}=\mathcal{L}u^{(k)}+f\quad;\quad u^{(k)}(0)=u_{0}\,.

Then
u^{(1)}(t)=u^{(2)}(t)
in the sense of distribution, for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

We denote

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}X_{k}=\Psi_{k}^{\,\mu_{k}}\mathcal{H}_{p_{k},d-2}^{\gamma_{k}+2}(\Omega,T)\,\,,\,\,\,\,X=X_{1}\cap X_{2}\\
Y_{k}=\Psi_{k}^{\,\mu_{k}}\mathbb{H}_{p_{k},d+2p_{k}-2}^{\gamma_{k}}(\Omega,T)\times\Psi_{k}^{\,\mu_{k}}B_{p_{k},d}^{\gamma_{k}+2-2/p_{k}}(\Omega)\,\,,\,\,\,\,Y=Y_{1}\cap Y_{2}\,.\end{gathered}

Step 1.
We first consider the case
\mathcal{L}=\nu_{1}\Delta
.
For
(f,u_{0})\in Y
, by Lemmas
4.10
and
4.5
.(1) (the counterpart of Lemma
3.10
.(1)), there exists
(f_{n},u_{0,n})\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big((0,T]\times\Omega\big)\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that

(f_{n},u_{0,n})\rightarrow(f,u_{0})\quad\text{in}\quad Y\,.

Since
\mu_{k}\in(-1/p_{k},1-1/p_{k})
, it follows from Lemmas
4.4
and
4.15
that there exists
u_{n}\in X=X_{1}\cap X_{2}
such that

\partial_{t}u_{n}=\nu_{1}\Delta u_{n}+f_{n}\quad;\quad u_{n}(0)=u_{0,n}\,.

By Theorem
4.12
, we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|u_{n}-u^{(k)}\|_{X_{k}}\lesssim\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|(f_{n},u_{0,n})-(f,u_{0})\|_{Y_{k}}=0

for each
k=1,\,2
.
Due to (
4.17
) and that
u_{0,n}\rightarrow u_{0}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
(see the counterpart of Lemma
3.12
.(2)), we obtain that

u^{(1)}(t)=u^{(2)}(t)\quad\text{in}\,\,\,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)\,\,,\,\,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,t\in[0,T]\,.

Therefore the case
\mathcal{L}=\nu_{1}\Delta
is proved.

We can also observe that

u^{(1)}(\,\cdot\,)
(
=u^{(2)}(\,\cdot\,)
) is the unique solution of the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\nu_{1}\Delta u+f\quad;\quad u(0)=u_{0}\,,

(4.24)

in the class
X
.
This is because
X_{1}\cap X_{2}
and
X_{1}
admits the unique solution to the equation (
4.24
), and
X_{1}\cap X_{2}\subset X_{1}
.

Step 2.
Let
\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{M}_{T}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
.
For
r\in[0,1]
, denote
\mathcal{L}_{r}:=(1-r)\nu_{1}\Delta+r\mathcal{L}
.
Due to (
4.23
), Theorem
4.12
implies that

\|u\|_{X}=\|u\|_{X_{1}}+\|u\|_{X_{2}}\leq N\|\big(\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{L}_{r}u,u(0)\big)\|_{Y}\quad\text{for all}\,\,\,u\in X

where
N
is independent of
u
and
r\in[0,1]
.
In addition, by the result in Step 1, the map
u\mapsto\big(\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{L}_{0}u,u(0)\big)
is a bijective map from
X
to
Y
.
Therefore the method of continuity yields that for any
(f,u_{0})\in Y
, there exists a unique solution
u\in X=X_{1}\cap X_{2}
of the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\mathcal{L}u+f\quad;\quad u(0)=u_{0}\,.

(4.25)

For each
k=1,\,2
,
u^{(k)}
is the unique solution of equation (
4.25
) in
X_{k}
, which implies
u=u^{(k)}
.
Consequently,
u^{(1)}(t)=u(t)=u^{(2)}(t)
for all
t\in(0,T]
.
∎

5.
Application I - Domain with fat exterior or thin exterior

In this section, we introduce applications of Sections
3
and
4
to domains satisfying fat exterior or thin exterior conditions. The notions of fat exterior and thin exterior are closely related to the geometry of a domain
\Omega
, namely the Hausdorff dimension and Aikawa dimension of
\Omega^{c}
.

For a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the Hausdorff dimension of
E
is defined by

\dim_{\mathcal{H}}(E):=\inf\big\{\lambda\geq 0\,:\,H^{\lambda}_{\infty}(E)=0\big\}\,,

where

\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{\lambda}(E):=\inf\Big\{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}r_{i}^{\lambda}\,:\,E\subset\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}B(x_{i},r_{i})\quad\text{where }x_{i}\in E\text{ and }r_{i}>0\Big\}\,.

The Aikawa dimension of
E
, denoted by
\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(E)
, is defined by the infimum of
\beta\geq 0
for which

\sup_{p\in E,\,r>0}\frac{1}{r^{\beta}}\int_{B_{r}(p)}\frac{1}{d(x,E)^{d-\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\,,

with considering
\frac{1}{0}=+\infty
.

Remark 5.1
.

(i) The Aikawa dimension is defined through integration.
However, this dimension equals the Assouad dimension (see
[
55
, Theorem 1.1]
).
The Assouad dimension is defined in terms of a covering property, similar to the Hausdorff dimension and Minkowski dimension.
Specifically, the Assouad dimension of a set
E
is the infimum of
\beta\geq 0
for which there exists
N_{\beta}>0
such that, for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, each subset
F\subset E
can be covered by at most
N_{\beta}\epsilon^{-\beta}
balls of radius
r=\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{diam}(F)
.

(ii) For any
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\dim_{\mathcal{H}}(E)\leq\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(E)

and the equality does not hold in general (see
[
54
, Section 2.2]
).
However, if
E
is Alfors regular, for example, if
E
has a self-similar property such as Cantor set or Koch snowflake set, then
\dim_{\mathcal{H}}(E)
equals
\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(E)
; see
[
54
, Lemma 2.1]
and
[
60
, Theorem 4.14]
.

Koskela and Zhong
[
41
]
established the dimensional dichotomy results for domains admitting the Hardy inequality, using the Hausdorff and Minkowski dimension.
Their result can be expressed through Hausdorff and Aikawa dimension, as shown in
[
54
, Theorem 5.3]
.

Proposition 5.2

(see Theorem 5.3 of
[
54
]
)
.

Suppose a domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
admits the Hardy inequality.
Then there is a constant
\epsilon>0
such that for each
p\in\partial\Omega
and
r>0
, either

\dim_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\Omega^{c}\cap\overline{B}(p,4r)\big)\geq d-2+\epsilon\quad\text{or}\quad\dim_{\mathcal{A}}\big(\Omega^{c}\cap\overline{B}(p,r)\big)\leq d-2-\epsilon\,.

For a deeper discussion of the dimensional dichotomy, we refer the reader to
[
72
]
.
In virtue of Proposition
5.2
, we consider domains
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
which satisfy one of the following situations:

(1)

(Fat exterior) There exists
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{d-2+\epsilon}_{\infty}\big(\Omega^{c}\cap\overline{B}(p,r)\big)\geq cr^{d-2+\epsilon}\quad\text{for all }p\in\partial\Omega\,\,,\,\,\,\,r>0\,.

(5.1)

(2)

(Thin exterior)
\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(\Omega^{c})<d-2
.

It is mentioned in detail in Subsections
5.1
and
5.3
that if a domain satisfyies one of these situations, then this domain admits the Hardy inequality.

In this section and Section
6
, for various domains
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we construct superharmonic functions equivalent to powers of boundary distance functions
\rho^{\alpha}:=d(\,\cdot\,,\partial\Omega)^{\alpha}
.
It is provided in Remark
5.4
that for each
p\in(1,\infty)
, these superharmonic functions imply ranges of
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
for which the following statement holds:

Statement 5.3

(
\Omega,p,\theta
)
.

For any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, the following hold:

(1)

For any
\lambda\geq 0
and
f\in H_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega)
, the equation

\Delta u-\lambda u=f

has a unique solution
u
in
H^{\gamma+2}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{H^{\gamma+2}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)}+\lambda\|u\|_{H_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\leq N_{1}\|f\|_{H_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\,,

(5.2)

where
N_{1}
is a constant independent of
u
,
f
, and
\lambda
.

(2)

Let
T\in(0,\infty]
.
For any
u_{0}\in B^{\gamma+2-2/p}_{p,\theta+2}(\Omega)
and
f\in\mathbb{H}_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega,T)
, the equation

\displaystyle u_{t}=\Delta u+f\quad\text{on }\Omega\times(0,T]\quad;\quad u(0,\cdot)=u_{0}\,.

has a unique solution
u
in
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+2}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)
.
Morever, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+2}_{p,\theta}(\Omega)}\leq N_{2}\big(\|u_{0}\|_{B^{\gamma+2-2/p}_{p,\theta+2}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{H_{p,\theta+2p}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\big)\,,

(5.3)

where
N_{2}
is a constant independent of
u
,
f
, and
T
.

Remark 5.4
.

Let
\Omega
admit the Hardy inequality (
1.3
) and suppose that for a fixed
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, there exists a superharmonic function
\psi
and a constant
M>0
such that

M^{-1}\rho^{\alpha}\leq\psi\leq M\rho^{\alpha}\,.

Then
\psi
is a superharmonic Harnack function, and
\Psi:=\widetilde{\rho}^{\,\alpha}
is a regularization of
\psi
.
Furthermore, the constants
\mathrm{C}_{2}(\Psi)
and
\mathrm{C}_{3}(\Psi,\psi)
can be chosen to depend only on
d
,
\alpha
and
M
.
In this case, Lemmas
3.10
.(3) and
4.5
.(1) (the counterpart of Lemma
3.10
.(3)) imply that for any
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\gamma,\,\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
N=N(d,p,\gamma,\alpha,\mu,M)
such that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\Psi^{\mu}H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\simeq_{N}\|f\|_{H_{p,\theta-\alpha\mu}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\quad\text{and}\quad\|f\|_{\Psi^{\mu}B_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\simeq_{N}\|f\|_{B_{p,\theta-\alpha\mu}^{\gamma}(\Omega)}\,.

Therefore, due to Theorems
3.18
and
4.12
(with Proposition
4.2
.(1)), we conclude that Statement
5.3

(\Omega,p,\theta)
holds for all
p\in(1,\infty)
and

\displaystyle\theta\in\big(d-2-(p-1)\alpha,d-2+\alpha\big)\quad\text{if}\,\,\alpha>0\,;

\displaystyle\theta\in\big(d-2+\alpha,d-2-(p-1)\alpha\big)\quad\text{if}\,\,\alpha<0\,.

Moreover,
N_{1}
(in (
5.2
)) and
N_{2}
(in (
5.3
)) depend only
d,\,p,\,\gamma,\,\theta,\,\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega),\,\alpha
and
M
.

We collect basic properties of classical superharmonic functions, which are used in this section and Section
6
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\Omega
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(1)

Let
\phi_{1},\,\phi_{2}
be classical superharmonic functions on
\Omega
. Then
\phi_{1}\wedge\phi_{2}
is also a classical superharmonic function on
\Omega
.

(2)

Let
\{\phi_{\alpha}\}
be a family of positive classical superharmonic functions on
\Omega
.
Then
\phi:=\inf_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}
is a superharmonic function on
\Omega
.

(3)

Let
\phi_{1},\,\phi_{2}
be positive classical superharmonic functions on
\Omega
. For any
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\phi_{1}^{\alpha}\phi_{2}^{1-\alpha}
is also a classical superharmonic fucntion on
\Omega
; in particular,
\phi_{1}^{\alpha}
is a classical superharmonic function for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

(4)

Let
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
be open sets in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\phi_{i}
be a classical superharmonic function on
\Omega_{i}
, for
i=1,\,2
.
Suppose that

\displaystyle\liminf_{x\rightarrow x_{1},x\in\Omega_{2}}\phi_{2}(x)\geq\phi_{1}(x_{1})\quad

\displaystyle\text{for all}\quad x_{1}\in\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2}\,;

\displaystyle\liminf_{x\rightarrow x_{2},x\in\Omega_{1}}\phi_{1}(x)\geq\phi_{2}(x_{2})\quad

\displaystyle\text{for all}\quad x_{2}\in\Omega_{2}\cap\partial\Omega_{1}\,.

Then the function

\displaystyle\phi(x):=\begin{cases}\phi_{1}(x)&\quad x\in\Omega_{1}\setminus\Omega_{2}\\
\phi_{1}(x)\wedge\phi_{2}(x)&\quad x\in\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}\\
\phi_{2}(x)&\quad x\in\Omega_{2}\setminus\Omega_{1}\\
\end{cases}

is also a classical superharmonic function on
\Omega
.

For the proof of Proposition
5.5
, (1) follows from the definition of classical superharmonic functions, (2) and (3) can be found in
[
7
, Theorem 3.7.5, Corollary 3.4.4]
, respectively, and (4) is implied by
[
7
, Corollary 3.2.4]
.

5.1.
Domain with fat exterior : Harmonic measure decay property

This subsection begins by introducing a relation among the condition (
5.1
), classical potential theory, and the Hardy inequality; see the paragraph below Remark
5.11
.

We first recall notions in classical potential theory.
For a bounded open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
d\geq 2
) and a bounded Borel function
f
on
\partial U
, the Perron-Wiener-Brelot solution (abbreviated to ‘PWB solution’) of the equation

\displaystyle\Delta u=0\quad\text{in}\,\,\,U\quad;\quad u=f\quad\text{on}\,\,\,\partial U

(5.4)

is defined by

\displaystyle u(x):=\inf\big\{\phi(x)\,:\,\text{$\phi$ is a superharmonic function on $U$ and}

(5.5)

\displaystyle\text{$\underset{y\rightarrow z}{\liminf}\,\phi(y)\geq f(z)$ for all $z\in\partial U$}

\displaystyle\big\}\,.

For a Borel set
E\subset\partial U
,
w(\,\cdot\,,U,E)
denotes the PWB solution
u
of the equation

\displaystyle\Delta u=0\quad\text{in}\,\,\,U\quad;\quad u=1_{E}\quad\text{on}\,\,\,\partial U\,,

which is also called the
harmonic measure
of
E
over
U
.

Remark 5.6
.

A bounded open set
U
is said to be regular if, for any
f\in C(\partial U)
, the PWB solution of equation (
5.4
) belongs to
C(\overline{U})
and satisfies (
5.4
) pointwisely.
One of the equivalent conditions for
U
to be regular is provided by N. Wiener
[
73
]
(see with
[
7
, Theorem 7.7.2]
), which is called the Wiener criterion.

We fix an arbitrary open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\geq 2
(not necessarily bounded).
For
p\in\partial\Omega
and
r>0
, we denote

w(\,\cdot\,,p,r)=w\big(\,\cdot\,,\Omega\cap B_{r}(p),\Omega\cap\partial B_{r}(p)\big)

(see Figure
5.1
below); note that
\Omega\cap\partial B_{r}(p)
is a relatively open subset of
\partial\big(\Omega\cap B_{r}(p)\big)
.

\Delta u=0
u=1
u=0

Figure 5.1.
u:=w(\,\cdot\,,p,r)

Here are basic properties of
w(\,\cdot\,,p,r)
which can be found in
[
7
, Chapter 6]
.

Proposition 5.7
.

(1)

w(\,\cdot\,,p,r)
is harmonic on
\Omega\cap B_{r}(p)
with values in
[0,1]
.

(2)

For any
x_{0}\in\Omega\cap\partial B_{r}(p)
,
\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\rightarrow x_{0}\\
x\in\Omega\cap B_{r}(p)\end{subarray}}{\lim}w(x,p,r)=1
.

For convenience, based on Proposition
5.7
, we consider
w(\,\cdot\,,p,r)
to be continuous on
\Omega\cap\overline{B}(p,r)
with
w(x,p,r)=1
for
x\in\Omega\cap\partial B(p,r)
.

Definition 5.8
.

A domain
\Omega
is said to satisfy the
local harmonic measure decay property
with exponent
\alpha>0
(abbreviated to ‘
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
’), if there exists a constant
M_{\alpha}>0
depending only on
\Omega
and
\alpha
such that

\displaystyle w(x,p,r)\leq M_{\alpha}\left(\frac{|x-p|}{r}\right)^{\alpha}\quad\text{for all}\,\,\,x\in\Omega\cap B(p,r)

(5.6)

whenever
p\in\partial\Omega
and
r>0
.

It is worth noting that if
\Omega
satisfies
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha>0
, then
\Omega
is regular (see,
e.g.
,
[
7
, Theorem 6.6.4]
).

Remark 5.9
.

\mathbf{LHMD}
is closely related to the Hölder continuity of the PWB solutions.
We temporarily assume that
\Omega
is a bounded regular domain (see Remark
5.6
).
For
\alpha\in(0,1]
and
f\in C(\partial\Omega)
, by
H_{\Omega}f
we denote the PWB solution
u
of the equation

\Delta u=0\quad\text{on}\,\,\,\Omega\,\,\,;\quad u|_{\partial\Omega}\equiv f\,,

and denote

\|H_{\Omega}\|_{\alpha}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in C^{0,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)\\
f\not\equiv 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\|H_{\Omega}f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Omega)}}{\|f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(\partial\Omega)}}\,.

The following are provided in
[
4
, Theorem 2, Theorem 3]
:

(1)

\|H_{\Omega}\|_{1}=\infty
.

(2)

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
, if
\|H_{\Omega}\|_{\alpha}<\infty
then
\Omega
satisfies
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
.
Conversely, if
\Omega
satisfies
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha^{\prime})
for some
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
, then
\|H_{\Omega}\|_{\alpha}<\infty
.

Remark 5.10
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain, and suppose that for a constant
\alpha>0
, there exist constants
r_{0},\,\widetilde{M}\in(0,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle w(x,p,r)\leq\widetilde{M}\left(\frac{|x-p|}{r}\right)^{\alpha}\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in\Omega\cap B(p,r)

whenever
p\in\partial\Omega
and
r\in(0,r_{0}]
.
Then
\Omega
satisfies
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
, where
M_{\alpha}
in (
5.6
) depends only on
\alpha,\,\widetilde{M}
and
\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)/r_{0}
.
Indeed, for a fixed
p\in\partial\Omega
, the function

w(x,p,r_{0})1_{\Omega\cap B(p,r_{0})}+1_{\Omega\setminus B(p,r_{0})}

is a classical superharmonic function on
\Omega
(see Propositions
5.5
.(4) and
5.7
).
In addition, the definition of harmonic measures implies the following:

\displaystyle\text{If $r>r_{0}$ then }w(\,\cdot\,,p,r)\leq w(\,\cdot\,,p,r_{0})\text{ on $\Omega\cap B(p,r_{0})$,; }

\displaystyle\text{If $r\geq\text{diam}(\Omega)$, then $w(\,\cdot\,,p,r)\equiv 0$}.

Therefore for any
r>0
,

w(x,p,r)\leq\widetilde{M}\left(\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{r_{0}}\vee 1\right)^{\alpha}\left(\frac{|x-p|}{r}\right)^{\alpha}\quad\text{for all}\,\,\,x\in\Omega\cap B(p,r)\,.

Remark 5.11
.

For an open ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and compact set
K\subset B
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cap}(K,B):=\inf\left\{\|\nabla f\|_{2}^{2}\,:\,f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B)\,\,\,,\,\,\,f\geq 1\,\,\text{on}\,\,K\right\},

(5.7)

denotes the capacity of
K
relative to
B
.
Ancona establishes the following in
[
6
, Lemma 3, Theorem 1, Theorem 2]
:

(1)

\Omega
satisfies
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
if and only if there exists
\epsilon_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\inf_{p\in\partial\Omega,r>0}\frac{\mathrm{Cap}(\Omega^{c}\cap\overline{B}(p,r),B(p,2r))}{r^{d-2}}\geq\epsilon_{0}>0\,.

(5.8)

Here,
\epsilon_{0}
and
(\alpha,M_{\alpha})
depend only on each other and
d
.

(2)

If
\Omega
satisfies (
5.8
), then the Hardy inequality (
1.3
) holds for
\Omega
, where
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega)
depends only on
d
and
\epsilon_{0}
.

(3)

If
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a planar domain and admits the Hardy inequality, then (
5.8
) holds for some
\epsilon_{0}
.

The condition (
5.8
) is also called the capacity density condition or uniformly fat exterior condition.
A well-known sufficient condition to satisfy (
5.8
) is

\displaystyle\inf_{p\in\partial\Omega,r>0}\frac{m\big(\Omega^{c}\cap\overline{B}(p,r)\big)}{r^{d}}\geq\epsilon_{1}>0\,,

(5.9)

where
m
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Indeed, if
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big(B(p,2r)\big)
satisfies
f\geq 1
on
\Omega^{c}\cap\overline{B}(p,r)
, then the Poincaré inequality implies

r^{-d+2}\int_{B(p,2r)}|\nabla f|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\gtrsim_{d}r^{-d}\int_{B(p,2r)}|f|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\geq\frac{m\big(\Omega^{c}\cap\overline{B}(p,r)\big)}{r^{d}}\,.

Therefore, (
5.9
) implies (
5.8
), where
\epsilon_{0}
depends only on
d
and
\epsilon_{1}
.

For a deeper discussion of the capacity density condition, we refer the reader to
[
40
,
56
]
and the references given therein.

We finally introduce the relation between (
5.1
) and the local harmonic measure decay property.
It was established by Lewis
[
56
, Theorem 1]
that if
\Omega
satisfies the capacity density condition (
5.8
), then there exist constants
c,\,\epsilon>0
depending only on
d,\,\epsilon_{0}
such that

\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{d-2+\epsilon}\big(\Omega^{c}\cap\overline{B}(p,r)\big)\geq c\,r^{d-2+\epsilon}

for all
p\in\partial\Omega
and
r>0
.
Conversely, it is well known (see,
e.g.
,
[
3
, Theorem B]
or
[
7
, Theorem 5.9.6]
) that
for any
\epsilon>0
and a compact set
E\subset B_{1}(0)
, we have

\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{d-2+\epsilon}(E)\leq N(d,\epsilon)\cdot\mathrm{Cap}\big(E,B_{2}(0)\big)\,.

Therefore, due to Remark
5.11
.(1), (
5.1
) holds for some
\epsilon,\,c>0
if and only if
\Omega
satisfies
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha>0
.

Based on this discussion, we consider domains satisfying
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha>0
, instead of (
5.1
).
This condition is implied by geometric conditions introduced in Section
6
, and the value of
\alpha
reflects each geometric condition; see Theorem
6.5
, Remark
6.9
, and Corollary
6.19
.
In the rest of this subsection, we construct appropriate superharmonic functions related to
\alpha
(see Remark
5.4
).
The results in this subsection are crucially used in Subsection
6
.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
\Omega
satisfy
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
,
\alpha>0
.
Then for any
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
, there exists a superharmonic function
\phi
on
\Omega
satisfying

N^{-1}\rho^{\beta}\leq\phi\leq N\rho^{\beta}

where
N=N(\alpha,\beta,M_{\alpha})>0
.

Before proving Theorem
5.12
, we look at the following corollary:

Corollary 5.13
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfy
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
,
\alpha>0
.
For any
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

d-2-(p-1)\alpha<\theta<d-2+\alpha\,,

Statement
5.3

(\Omega,p,\theta)
holds.
In addition,
N_{0}
(in (
5.2
)) and
N_{1}
(in (
5.3
)) depends only on
d
,
p
,
\gamma
,
\theta
,
\alpha
,
M_{\alpha}
.

Proof of Corollary
5.13
.

Take
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
such that

d-2-(p-1)\beta<\theta<d-2+\beta.

It follows from Theorem
5.12
that there exists a superharmonic function
\phi
such that

N^{-1}\rho^{\beta}\leq\phi\leq N\rho^{\beta}\,,

where
N=N(\alpha,\,\beta,\,M_{\alpha})
.
Remarks
5.11
.(1) and (2) yield that
\Omega
admits the Hardy inequality (
1.3
), where
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega)
can be chosen to depend only on
d,\alpha
and
M_{\alpha}
(in (
5.6
)).
Therefore, by Remark
5.4
, the proof is completed.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.12
.

The following construction is a combination of
[
6
, Theorem 1]
and
[
32
, Lemma 2.1]
.
Recall that
M_{\alpha}
is the constant in (
5.6
), and
\beta<\alpha
.
Take
r_{0}\in(0,1)
small enough to satisfy
M_{\alpha}r_{0}^{\alpha}<r_{0}^{\beta}
, and take
\eta\in(0,1)
small enough to satisfy

(1-\eta)M_{\alpha}r_{0}^{\alpha}+\eta\leq r_{0}^{\beta}\,.

For
w(x,p,r)
, we shall need only the following properties (see Proposition
5.7
and Definition
5.8
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{$w(\cdot,p,r)$ is a classical superharmonic function on $\Omega\cap B(p,r)$}\,;\\
&\text{$w(\cdot,p,r)=1$ on $\Omega\cap\partial B(p,r)$}\,;\\
&\text{$0\leq w(\cdot,p,r)\leq M_{\alpha}r_{0}^{\alpha}$ on $\Omega\cap B(p,r_{0}r)$}\,.\end{split}

For
p\in\partial\Omega
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, put

\phi_{p,k}(x)=r_{0}^{k\beta}\big((1-\eta)\,w(x,p,r_{0}^{k})+\eta\big).

Then
\phi_{p,k}
is a classical superharmonic function on
\Omega\cap B(p,r_{0}^{k})
,

\displaystyle\quad\,\,\phi_{p,k}\leq r_{0}^{(k+1)\beta}\qquad\,\,

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad\Omega\cap\overline{B}(p,r_{0}^{k+1})\,,

\displaystyle\quad\,\,\phi_{p,k}=r_{0}^{k\beta}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad\Omega\cap\partial B(p,r_{0}^{k})\,,

\displaystyle\eta\cdot r_{0}^{k\beta}\leq\phi_{p,k}\leq r_{0}^{k\beta}\quad\,\,

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad\Omega\cap B(p,r_{0}^{k})\,.

For
p\in\partial\Omega
and
x\in\Omega
, we denote

\phi_{p}(x)=\inf\{\phi_{p,k}(x)\,:\,|x-p|<r_{0}^{k}\}.

If we prove the following:

\phi_{p}
is a classical superharmonic function on
\Omega
;

(5.10)

\displaystyle\eta|x-p|^{\beta}\leq\phi_{p}(x)\leq r_{0}^{-\beta}|x-p|^{\beta}\,,

(5.11)

then
\phi:=\inf_{p\in\partial\Omega}\phi_{p}
is superharmonic on
\Omega
(see Proposition
5.5
.(2)) and satisfies

\eta\rho(x)^{\beta}\leq\phi(x)\leq r_{0}^{-\beta}\rho(x)^{\beta}\,.

Therefore the proof is completed.

To obtain (
5.10
) and (
5.11
), we only need to prove each of the following, respectively: for each
k_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\phi_{p}
is a classical superharmonic function on
\{x\in\Omega\,:\,r_{0}^{k_{0}+2}<|x-p|<r_{0}^{k_{0}}\}
;

\eta\,r_{0}^{k_{0}\beta}\leq\phi_{p}\leq r_{0}^{k_{0}\beta}
on
\{x\in\Omega\,:\,r_{0}^{k_{0}+1}\leq|x-p|<r_{0}^{k_{0}}\}.

-
(
5.10
) :
For
x\in\Omega\cap B(p,r_{0}^{k_{0}})
, put

\displaystyle v_{p,k_{0}}(x)=\begin{cases}\phi_{p,k_{0}}(x)&\text{if}\quad r_{0}^{k_{0}+1}\leq|x-p|<r_{0}^{k_{0}}\\
\phi_{p,k_{0}}(x)\wedge\phi_{p,k_{0}+1}(x)&\text{if}\quad|x-p|<r_{0}^{k_{0}+1}\,.\end{cases}

Since
\phi_{p,k_{0}}\leq\phi_{p,k_{0}+1}
on
\Omega\cap\partial B(p,r_{0}^{k_{0}+1})
, Proposition
5.5
.(4) implies that
v_{p,k_{0}}
is a classical superharmonic function on
\Omega\cap B(p,r_{0}^{k_{0}})
.
We denote

U_{k_{0}}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,r_{0}^{k_{0}+2}<|x-p|<r_{0}^{k_{0}}\}\,.

For
x\in U_{k_{0}}
, we have

\phi_{p}(x)=v_{p,k_{0}}(x)\wedge\inf\{\phi_{p,k}(x)\,:\,k\leq k_{0}-1\}.

Moreover, if
\eta\,r_{0}^{k\beta}\geq r_{0}^{k_{0}\beta}
then

v_{p,k_{0}}(x)\leq\phi_{p,k_{0}}(x)\leq r_{0}^{k_{0}\beta}\leq\eta\,r_{0}^{k\beta}\leq\phi_{p,k}(x)\,.

Therefore

\displaystyle\phi_{p}(x)=v_{p,k_{0}}(x)\wedge\inf\{\phi_{p,k}(x)\,:\,k\leq k_{0}-1\quad\text{and}\quad\eta\,r_{0}^{k\beta}\leq r_{0}^{k_{0}\beta}\}\,,

which implies that
\phi_{p}
is the minimum of finitely many classical superharmonic functions, on
U_{k_{0}}
.
Consequently, by Proposition
5.5
.(1),
\phi_{p}
is a classical superharmonic function on
U_{k_{0}}
.

-
(
5.11
) :
Let
x\in\Omega
satisfy
r_{0}^{k_{0}+1}\leq|x-p|<r_{0}^{k_{0}}
.
Since

\phi_{p,k_{0}}(x)\leq r_{0}^{k_{0}\beta}\,\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{p,k}(x)\geq\eta r_{0}^{k\beta}\geq\eta r_{0}^{k_{0}\beta}\quad\text{for all}\quad k\leq k_{0}\,,

we obtain that
\eta\,r_{0}^{k_{0}\beta}\leq\phi_{p}(x)\leq r_{0}^{k_{0}\beta}
.
∎

5.2.
Further results for domains with fat exterior

In this subsection, we introduce a unweighted solvablity results and embedding theorems for the Poisson and heat equations in domains satisfying the capacity density condition (
5.8
).

Recall that
\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)
denotes the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in

W_{p}^{1}(\Omega):=\big\{f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)\,:\,\|f\|_{W_{p}^{1}(\Omega)}:=\|f\|_{p}+\|\nabla f\|_{p}<\infty\big\}\,.

Note that
W_{p}^{1}(\Omega)
is a Banach space, and therefore
\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)
is also a Banach space.

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
\Omega
satisfy the capacity density condition (
5.8
) and

\displaystyle\lambda\geq 0\quad\text{if}\quad d_{\Omega}<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda>0\quad\text{if}\quad d_{\Omega}=\infty\,,

(5.12)

where
d_{\Omega}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}d(x,\partial\Omega)
.
Then there exists
\epsilon\in(0,1)
depending only on
d
,
\epsilon_{0}
(in (
5.8
)) such that for any
p\in(2-\epsilon,2+\epsilon)
, the following holds:

For any
f^{0},\,\ldots,\,f^{d}\in L_{p}(\Omega)
, the equation

\displaystyle\Delta u-\lambda u=f^{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}f^{i}

(5.13)

has a unique solution
u
in
\mathring{W}^{1}_{p}(\Omega)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|\nabla u\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}+\big(\lambda^{1/2}+d_{\Omega}^{-1}\big)\|u\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}\\
\leq N(d,p,\epsilon_{0})\,&\Big(\min\big(\lambda^{-1/2},d_{\Omega}\big)\|f^{0}\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|f^{i}\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}\Big)\,.\end{split}

(5.14)

Proof.

We first note the following two result which follows from (
5.8
):

(a)

By Remark
5.11
.(1), there exists
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that
\Omega
satisfies
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
.
Due to Corollary
5.13
, Statement
5.3

(\Omega,p,d-p)
holds for

\displaystyle 2-\alpha<p<2+\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\,,

and
N_{1}
(in (
5.2
)) depends only on
d,p,\gamma,\epsilon_{1}
.

(b)

It is implied by
[
56
, Theorem 1, Theorem 2]
(or see
[
40
, Theorem 3.7, Corollary 3.11]
) that there exists
p_{0}\in(1,2)
depending only on
d
and
\epsilon_{0}
such that for any
p>p_{0}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\Big|\frac{u(x)}{\rho(x)}\Big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq N(d,p,\epsilon_{0})\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\forall\,\,u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)\,.

(5.15)

Due to Lemma
3.12
.(1) and the definition of
\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)
,
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is dense in
\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)
and
H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
, separately. Therefore (
5.15
) implies that
\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)\subset H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
.

Take
\epsilon\in(0,1)
such that
\epsilon\leq\alpha
and
\epsilon\leq 2-p_{0}
.
We consider a fixed
p\in(2-\epsilon,2+\epsilon)
.

We will use Lemma
3.8
, Corollary
3.16
,
d_{\Omega}^{\,-1}\|u\|_{p}\leq\|\rho^{-1}u\|_{p}
, and
\|\rho f\|_{p}\leq d_{\Omega}\|f\|_{p}
, without mentioning.

Step 1. Uniqueness of solutions.

Suppose that
u\in\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)
satisfies
\Delta u-\lambda u=0
.
By (a) in this proof,
u
belongs to
H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
, which implies
u\equiv 0
.
Therefore, the uniqueness of solutions is proved.

Step 2. Existence of solutions and estimate (
5.14
).

To prove the existence of solutions, it is enough to find a solution in
L_{p,d}(\Omega)\cap H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
.
Indeed, if
u\in L_{p,d}(\Omega)\cap H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
, then there exists
u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that
u_{n}\rightarrow u
in
L_{p,d}(\Omega)\cap H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
(see Lemma
3.12
.(5)).
Therefore,

\|u_{n}-u\|_{W_{p}^{1}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|u_{n}-u\|_{L_{p,d}(\Omega)}+\|u_{n}-u\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)}\rightarrow 0\,,

which implies that
u\in\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)
.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\lambda=0
or
\lambda=1
by dilation.
Note that
\epsilon_{0}
in (
5.8
) is invariant even if
\Omega
is replaced by
r\Omega=\{rx\,:\,x\in\Omega\}
, for any
r>0
.

- Step 2.1.
Consider the case
\lambda=1
.
Since
\widetilde{\rho}^{-1}f^{0}\in L_{d+p}(\Omega)
and Statement
5.3

(\Omega,p,d-p)
holds, there exists
v\in H_{p,d-p}^{2}(\Omega)
such that

\Delta v-v=\widetilde{\rho}^{\,-1}f^{0}

and
\|v\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{2}(\Omega)}+\|v\|_{L_{p,d+p}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{d,p,\epsilon_{0}}\left\|\widetilde{\rho}^{-1}f^{0}\right\|_{L_{p,d+p}(\Omega)}

(see (a) in this proof)
By Proposition
A.3
.(9) and Lemma
3.10
.(1), we have

\displaystyle\|v\|_{L_{p,d}(\Omega)}+\left(\|v\|_{L_{p,d+p}(\Omega)}+\|v\|_{H_{p,d}^{1}(\Omega)}\right)

(5.16)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p}\,

\displaystyle\left(\|v\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)}+\|v\|_{H_{p,d+p}^{-1}(\Omega)}\right)+\left(\|v\|_{L_{p,d+p}(\Omega)}+\|v\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p}

\displaystyle\|v\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{2}(\Omega)}+\|v\|_{L_{p,d+p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p,\epsilon_{0}}

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\rho}^{-1}f^{0}\right\|_{L_{p,d+p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\simeq_{d,p}

\displaystyle\|f^{0}\|_{p}\,.

Observe that
\widetilde{f}:=f^{0}-\Delta(\widetilde{\rho}v)+\widetilde{\rho}v
satisfies

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}=-2\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}\big(vD_{i}\widetilde{\rho})\Big]+v\Delta\widetilde{\rho}

and therefore

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|\widetilde{f}\big\|_{H_{p,d+p}^{-1}(\Omega)}\,&\lesssim_{d,p}\left\|v\widetilde{\rho}_{x}\right\|_{L_{p,d}(\Omega)}+\left\|v\widetilde{\rho}_{xx}\right\|_{L_{p,d+p}(\Omega)}\\
&\lesssim_{d,p,\epsilon_{0}}\|v\|_{L_{p,d}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{d,p,\epsilon_{0}}\left\|f^{0}\right\|_{p}\,,\end{split}

(5.17)

where the last inequality follows from (
5.16
).
Since Statement
5.3

(\Omega,p,d-p)
holds, there exists
w\in H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
such that

\Delta w-w=\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}f^{i}+\widetilde{f}

and

\|w\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)}+\|w\|_{H^{-1}_{p,d+p}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{d,p,\epsilon_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|f^{i}\|_{L_{p,d}(\Omega)}+\big\|\widetilde{f}\big\|_{H_{p,d+p}^{-1}(\Omega)}\lesssim\sum_{i=0}^{d}\|f^{i}\|_{p}

(see (a) in this proof).
Therefore, by Proposition
A.3
.(9), Lemma
3.10
.(1), and (
5.17
), we have

\displaystyle\|w\|_{L_{p,d-p}(\Omega)}+\big(\|w\|_{L_{p,d}(\Omega)}+\|w\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)}\big)

(5.18)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p}

\displaystyle\|w\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)}+\|w\|_{H^{-1}_{p,d+p}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{d,p,\epsilon_{0}}\sum_{i\geq 0}\left\|f^{i}\right\|_{p}\,.

Put
u=v\widetilde{\rho}+w
.
Then
u
is a solution of equation (
5.13
) and satisfies

\displaystyle\|u_{x}\|_{p}+(1+d_{\Omega}^{-1})\|u\|_{p}

(5.19)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p}

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L_{p,d}(\Omega)}+\|u\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p}

\displaystyle\|w\|_{L_{p,d}(\Omega)}+\|w\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)}+\|v\|_{L_{p,d+p}(\Omega)}+\|v\|_{H_{p,d}^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p,\epsilon_{0}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 0}\|f^{i}\|_{p}\,,

Here, the second inequality follows from Lemma
3.10
.(3), and the last inequality follows from (
5.16
) and (
5.18
).

Note that (
5.19
) also implies that
u\in L_{p,d}(\Omega)\cap H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
.

- Step 2.2.
Consider the case
d_{\Omega}<\infty
, and observe that

\displaystyle\|f^{0}+\sum_{i\geq 1}D_{i}f^{i}\|_{H_{p,d+p}^{-1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p}\|f^{0}\|_{L_{p,d+p}(\Omega)}+\sum_{i\geq 1}\|f^{i}\|_{L_{p,d}(\Omega)}

(5.20)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\Omega}\|f^{0}\|_{p}+\sum_{i\geq 1}\|f^{i}\|_{p}<\infty\,,

Since Statement
5.3

(\Omega,p,d-p)
holds, either
\lambda=0
or
\lambda=1
, there exists
\widetilde{u}\in H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
such that

\Delta\widetilde{u}-\lambda\widetilde{u}=f^{0}+\sum_{i\geq 1}D_{i}f^{i}\,,

and

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{u}\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)}+\lambda\|\widetilde{u}\|_{H_{p,d+p}^{-1}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|f^{0}+\sum_{i\geq 1}D_{i}f^{i}\|_{H_{p,d+p}^{-1}(\Omega)}

(5.21)

(see (a) in this proof).
By Proposition
A.3
.(9), (
5.20
), and (
5.21
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\|\nabla\widetilde{u}\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}+d_{\Omega}^{-1}\|\widetilde{u}\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}+\lambda^{1/2}\|\widetilde{u}\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}

(5.22)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p}\,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{u}\|_{H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)}+\lambda\|\widetilde{u}\|_{H_{p,d+p}^{-1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,p,\epsilon_{0}}\,

\displaystyle d_{\Omega}\|f^{0}\|_{p}+\sum_{i\geq 1}\|f^{i}\|_{p}\,.

Due to (
5.22
), we have
\widetilde{u}\in L_{p,d}(\Omega)\cap H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
.

- Step 2.3.

The existence of solutions is proved in Steps 2.1 and 2.2, for all
\lambda
and
d_{\Omega}
satisfying (
5.12
).
For the cases where
d_{\Omega}=\infty
and
\lambda=1
, and
d_{\Omega}<\infty
and
\lambda=0
, estimate (
5.14
) is proved in (
5.19
) and (
5.22
), respectively.
Therefore, we only need prove estimate (
5.14
) in the remaining case where
d_{\Omega}<\infty
and
\lambda=1
.
Since
u
in Step 2.1 and
\widetilde{u}
in Step 2.2 are the same (due to the result in Step 1), (
5.23
) can be obtained by combining (
5.19
) and (
5.22
).
∎

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
\Omega
satisfy the capacity density condition (
5.8
) and

\displaystyle T\leq\infty\quad\text{if}\quad d_{\Omega}<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad T<\infty\quad\text{if}\quad d_{\Omega}=\infty\,,

where
d_{\Omega}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}d(x,\partial\Omega)
.
Then for any
\nu_{1},\,\nu_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
with
0<\nu_{1}\leq\nu_{2}<\infty
, there exists
\epsilon\in(0,1)
depending only on
d
,
\epsilon_{0}
(in (
5.8
)),
\nu_{1}
,
\nu_{2}
such that the following holds:

Suppose that
p\in(2-\epsilon,2+\epsilon)
and
\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{M}_{T}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
.
Then for any
f^{0},\,\ldots,\,f^{d}\in L_{p}((0,T]\times\Omega)
, the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\mathcal{L}u+f^{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}f^{i}\quad\text{in}\quad(0,T]\quad;\quad u(0,\cdot)=0

(5.23)

has a unique solution
u
in
L_{p}\big((0,T];\mathring{W}^{1}_{p}(\Omega)\big)
(see (
4.15
) for the definition of equation (
5.23
)).
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|\nabla u\|_{L_{p}((0,T]\times\Omega)}+\big(T^{-1/2}+(d_{\Omega})^{-1}\big)\|u\|_{L_{p}((0,T]\times\Omega)}\\
\leq N(d,p,\epsilon_{0})\,&\Big(\min(T^{1/2},d_{\Omega})\|f^{0}\|_{L_{p}((0,T]\times\Omega)}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|f^{i}\|_{L_{p}((0,T]\times\Omega)}\Big)\,.\end{split}

(5.24)

Proof.

We introduce the expression ‘
\mathrm{Statement}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\,(\Omega,p,\theta)
holds’ to indicate that

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\text{` Statement~\ref {22.02.19.1}}\,(\Omega,p,\theta).(2)\,\,\text{holds for $\Delta$ replaced by arbitary}\\
\text{$\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{M}_{T}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})$. In addition, $N_{2}$ (in \eqref{2205241156}) depends only}\\
\text{on $d$, $p$, $\theta$, $\epsilon_{0}$, $\nu_{1}$, $\nu_{2}$. '}\end{gathered}

Remarks
5.11
.(1), (2) and Theorem
5.12
imply the following:

-

\Omega
admits the Hardy inequality (
1.3
), where
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega)
can be chosen to depend only on
d
and
\epsilon_{0}
.

-

There exists
\alpha>0
and a superharmonic function
\phi
on
\Omega
such that

N^{-1}\rho^{\alpha}\leq\phi\leq N\rho^{\alpha}\,,

where
\alpha
and
N
depend only on
d
and
\epsilon_{0}
.

Therefore, due to Theorem
4.12
(with
\Psi=\widetilde{\rho}^{\alpha}
) and Proposition
4.2
.(1), if
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\displaystyle-\frac{(p-1)\alpha}{p(\sqrt{\nu_{2}/\nu_{1}}-1)/2+1}<\theta-d+2<\frac{(p-1)\alpha}{p(\sqrt{\nu_{2}/\nu_{1}}+1)/2-1}\,,

(5.25)

then Statement
{}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}(\Omega,p,\theta)
holds.
The first term in (
5.25
) goes to
-\alpha\sqrt{\nu_{1}/\nu_{2}}
as
p\rightarrow 2
, while the second term in (
5.25
) goes to
\alpha\sqrt{\nu_{1}/\nu_{2}}
as
p\rightarrow 2
.
Therefore, there exists
\epsilon_{1}>0
(which depends only on
\nu_{1}
,
\nu_{2}
, and
\alpha
) such that if
p\in(2-\epsilon_{1},2+\epsilon_{1})
, then
\theta:=d-p
satisfy (
5.25
), and thus
\mathrm{Statement}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\,(\Omega,p,d-p)
holds.

By (a) in the proof of Theorem
5.14
, there exists
p_{0}\in(1,2)
such that for any
p>p_{0}
,
\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)\subset H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
.

Take
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{1})
such that
2-\epsilon>p_{0}
.
Then for any
p\in(2-\epsilon,2+\epsilon)
,
\mathrm{Statement}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\,(\Omega,p,d-p)
holds and
\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)\subset H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
.

Step 1. Uniqueness of solutions.

Suppose that
u\in L_{p}((0,T];\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega))
satisfies

\partial_{t}u=\Delta u\quad;\quad u(0,\cdot)\equiv 0\,.

Since
\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)\subset H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
, we have
L_{p}((0,T];\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega))\subset\mathbb{H}_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega,T)
.
Therefore, by Lemma
4.15
,
u\in\mathcal{H}_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega,T)
.
Since Statement
{}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\,(\Omega,p,d-p)
holds,
u\equiv 0
.

Step 2. Existence of solutions and estimate (
5.24
).

Proof of the existence of solutions and estimate (
5.24
) is left to the reader, as it can be shown in a similar way by following Steps 2.1 - 2.3 in the proof of Theorem
5.14
, with the following details:

-

To prove the existence of solutions, it is enough to find a solution in
\mathbb{L}_{p,d}(\Omega,T)\cap\mathbb{H}_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega,T)
.
It is because if
u\in\mathbb{L}_{p,d}(\Omega,T)\cap\mathbb{H}_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega,T)
, there exists
u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega)
such that
u_{n}\rightarrow u
in
\mathbb{L}_{p,d}(\Omega,T)
and
\mathbb{H}_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega,T)
, separately (see Lemma
4.10
).
Since
L_{p,d}(\Omega)\cap H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)\subset\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)
(see Step 2 in the proof of Theorem
5.14
),
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L_{p}\big((0,T];\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)\big)
.
Therefore

u=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}u_{n}\quad\text{in}\,\,\,\,L_{p}\big((0,T];\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)\big)\,.

-

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
T=1
or
T=\infty
by dilation.

-

For the case
T=1
, note that if
v\in\mathcal{H}_{p,d-p}^{n+2}(\Omega,1)
satisfies
v(0)\equiv 0
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|v\|_{\mathbb{H}_{p,d+p}^{n}(\Omega,1)}^{p}&\,=\int_{0}^{1}\|v(t)\|_{H_{p,d+p}^{n}(\Omega)}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\|v_{t}(s)\|_{H_{p,d+p}^{n}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}s\Big)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\,\leq\|v_{t}\|_{\mathbb{H}_{p,d+p}^{n}(\Omega,1)}^{p}\leq\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}_{p,d-p}^{n+2}(\Omega,1)}\,.\end{split}

∎

Remark 5.16
.

Actually, from the proofs Theorem
5.14
and Theorem
5.15
, it can observed that for a fixed
p\in(1,\infty)
, the assertion in Theorem
5.14
(resp. Theorem
5.15
) holds if Statement
5.3

(\Omega,p,d-p)
holds (resp.
\mathrm{Statement}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\,(\Omega,p,d-p)
holds) and
\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)\subset H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
.
Note that if

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\partial\Omega\\
r>0\end{subarray}}\frac{m(\Omega^{c}\cap B_{r}(p))}{m(B_{r}(p))}>0

(where
m
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
),
then the
L_{p}
-Hardy inequality holds (see
[
40
, Example 3.6, Corollary 3.11]
), and therefore we hve
\mathring{W}_{p}^{1}(\Omega)\subset H_{p,d-p}^{1}(\Omega)
.

In the next theorems, we discuss the embedding theorems, Propositions
3.17
and
4.11
.
For a fixed
\epsilon\in(0,1]
, let
p
be large enough such that
p>d
and
\epsilon>1/p
.
Then it follows from Proposition
3.17
that if
f\in\Psi^{1-\epsilon}H_{p,d+2p-2}^{-1}(\Omega)
and
u\in\Psi^{1-\epsilon}H_{p,d-2}^{1}(\Omega)
satisfy
\Delta u=f
, then

\displaystyle u(x)\lesssim\|f\|_{\Psi^{1-\epsilon}H_{p,2p-2}^{-1}(\Omega)}\cdot\rho(x)^{-(d-2)/p}\Psi^{1-\epsilon}(x)\,.

In Theorems
5.17
and
5.18
, we modify this type of estimates to delete the term
\rho^{-(d-2)/p}
using Theorem
5.12
.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
\Omega
satisfy
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
,
\lambda\geq 0
, and
\psi
be a superharmonic Harnack function on
\Omega
.
Suppose that
\delta
and
\epsilon
are positive constants such that

\displaystyle 0<\delta<\frac{\alpha d}{\alpha+d-2}\wedge 1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\epsilon\in\begin{cases}(\delta/2,1+\delta/2)&\text{if}\quad d=2\,;\\[2.84526pt]
(\frac{\alpha+d-2}{\alpha d}\delta,1]&\text{if}\quad d\geq 3\,.\end{cases}

(5.26)

If
f^{0},\,f^{i},\,\ldots,\,f^{d}
are measurable functions on
\Omega
with

\displaystyle F:=\Big\||\psi^{-1+\epsilon}\rho^{2-\delta}f^{0}|+\sum_{i=1}^{d}|\psi^{-1+\epsilon}\rho^{1-\delta}f^{i}|\Big\|_{L_{d/\delta}(\Omega,\,\mathrm{d}x)}<\infty\,,

(5.27)

then the equation

\displaystyle\Delta u-\lambda u=f^{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}f^{i}

(5.28)

has a unique solution
u
in
\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}H_{d/\delta,0}^{1}(\Omega)
,
where
\widetilde{\psi}
is the regularization of
\psi
in Lemma
3.5
.(1).
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle|u(x)|+\rho(x)^{1-\delta}\sup_{y\in B_{\rho(x)/2}(x)}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|}{|x-y|^{1-\delta}}\leq NF\cdot\big(\psi(x)\big)^{1-\epsilon}

(5.29)

for all
x\in\Omega
, where
N=N(d,p,\mathrm{C}_{1}(\psi),\alpha,M_{\alpha},\delta,\epsilon)
.

Proof.

Put
p=d/\delta
, and note that Corollary
3.16
implies

\displaystyle\|f^{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}f^{i}\|_{\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}H^{-1}_{p,2p}(\Omega)}\lesssim F\,.

(5.30)

Case 1.

d=2
.

Observe that
\delta=2/p
and
1-\epsilon\in(-1/p,1-1/p)
.
Due to (
5.30
) and
d-2=0
,
this corollary is implied by Theorem
3.18
and Proposition
3.17
.

Case 2.

d\geq 3
.

Take
\alpha_{1}\in(0,\alpha)
such that

\epsilon>\frac{\alpha_{1}+d-2}{\alpha_{1}d}\delta\,,

and put

\mu=\frac{d-2}{\alpha_{1}\,p}+(1-\epsilon)\,\,,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,t=\mu^{-1}(1-\epsilon)\,,

so that

\mu\in(0,1-1/p)\,\,,\,\,\,\,t\in[0,1]\,\,,\,\,\,\,\mu t=1-\epsilon\,\,,\,\,\,\,\alpha_{1}\mu(1-t)=(d-2)/p.

By Theorem
5.12
, there exists a superharmonic function
\phi_{0}
such that
\phi_{0}\simeq_{N}\rho^{\alpha_{1}}
where
N=N(\alpha,M_{\alpha},\alpha_{1})
.
Put
\Psi=\widetilde{\rho}^{\,\alpha_{1}(1-t)}\big(\widetilde{\psi}\big)^{t}
which is a regularization of the superharmonic function
\phi_{0}^{1-t}\psi^{t}
(see Proposition
5.5
.(3)).
Note that, by Lemma
3.10
.(3),

\Psi^{\mu}H_{p,\theta+d-2}^{\gamma}(\Omega)=\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}H_{p,\theta}^{\gamma}(\Omega)

for all
\gamma,\,\theta\in\mathbb{R}
.
By (
5.30
) and Theorem
3.18
, equation (
5.28
) has a unique solution
u
in the class
\Psi^{\mu}H_{p,d-2}^{1}(\Omega)=\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}H_{p,0}^{1}(\Omega)
.
Furthermore, Proposition
3.17
implies (
5.29
) for this
u
.
∎

Theorem 5.18
.

Let
\Omega
satisfy
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
,
T\in(0,\infty)
, and
\psi
be a superharmonic Harnack function on
\Omega
. Suppose that
\beta_{x},\,\beta_{t},\,\delta
and
\epsilon
are constants in
(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\beta_{x}+2\beta_{t}\leq 1-\delta\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\delta}{d+2}+\alpha^{-1}\Big(\frac{d}{d+2}\delta+2\beta_{t}\Big)<\epsilon\leq 1\,.

(5.31)

If
f^{0},\,\ldots,\,f^{d}:(0,T]\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
u_{0}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are measurable functions satisfy

\displaystyle\Big\||\psi^{-1+\epsilon}\rho^{2-2\beta_{t}-\delta}f^{0}|+\sum_{i=1}^{d}|\psi^{-1+\epsilon}\rho^{1-2\beta_{t}-\delta}f^{i}|\Big\|_{L_{(d+2)/\delta}((0,T]\times\Omega,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t)}

\displaystyle+\Big\|\psi^{-1+\epsilon}\rho^{-2\beta_{t}-\delta}|u_{0}|+\psi^{-1+\epsilon}\rho^{1-2\beta_{t}-\delta}|\nabla u_{0}|\Big\|_{L_{d/\delta}(\Omega,\,\mathrm{d}x)}

\displaystyle=:\,

\displaystyle F+I<\infty\,,

then the equation

\displaystyle u_{t}=\Delta u+f^{0}+D_{i}f^{i}\quad;\quad u(0)=u_{0}

(5.32)

has a unique solution
u
in
\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}\mathcal{H}_{p,-2-2\beta_{t}p}^{1}(\Omega,T)
,
where
\widetilde{\psi}
is the regularization of
\psi
in Lemma
3.5
.(1).
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\big|\widetilde{\psi}^{-1+\epsilon}\big(u(t,\cdot)-u(s,\cdot)\big)\big|_{\beta_{x}}^{(0)}}{|t-s|^{\beta_{t}}}\leq N(F+I)

for all
s,\,t\in[0,T]
with
t>s
, where
N=N(d,p,\mathrm{C}_{1}(\psi),\alpha,M_{\alpha},\beta_{x},\beta_{t},\delta,\epsilon)
(see Proposition (
3.17
) for the definition of
|\cdot|_{\beta_{x}}^{(0)}
).

Proof.

Take
\alpha_{1}\in(0,\alpha)
such that

\epsilon>\frac{\delta}{d+2}+\alpha_{1}^{-1}\Big(\frac{d}{d+2}\delta+2\beta_{t}\Big)\,,

and put

p=\frac{d+2}{\delta}\,\,,\,\,\,\,\mu=\alpha_{1}^{-1}\Big(\,\frac{d}{p}+2\beta_{t}\,\Big)+(1-\epsilon)\,\,,\,\,\,\,t=\mu^{-1}(1-\epsilon)\,,

so that

\mu\in(0,1-1/p\big)\,\,,\,\,\,\,t\in[0,1]\,\,,\,\,\,\,\mu t=1-\epsilon\,\,,\,\,\,\,\alpha_{1}\mu(1-t)=\frac{d}{p}+2\beta_{t}.

By Theorem
5.12
, there exists a superharmonic function
s
satisfying
s\simeq\rho^{\alpha_{1}}
.
Put

\Psi=\widetilde{\rho}^{\,\alpha_{1}(1-t)}\widetilde{\psi}^{t}

which is a regularization of the superharmonic function
s^{1-t}\psi^{t}
(see Proposition
5.5
.(3)).
Note that

\displaystyle\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{1}(\Omega,T)\,

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}\mathcal{H}_{p,-2-2\beta_{t}p}^{1}(\Omega,T)\,,

\displaystyle\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{H}_{p,d+2p-2}^{-1}(\Omega,T)\,

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}\mathbb{H}_{p,-2+2(1-\beta_{t})p}^{-1}(\Omega,T)\,,

\displaystyle\quad\Psi^{\mu}B_{p,d}^{1-2/p}(\Omega)\,

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}B_{p,-2\beta_{t}p}^{1-2/p}(\Omega)\,.

Corollary
3.16
implies

\big\|f^{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}f^{i}\big\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathbb{H}_{p,d+2p-2}^{-1}(\Omega,T)}\simeq\big\|f^{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}f^{i}\big\|_{\widetilde{\psi}^{\,1-\epsilon}\mathbb{H}_{p,2p-2-2\beta_{t}p}^{-1}(\Omega,T)}\leq NF\,,

and Proposition
A.3
.(3) implies

\big\|u_{0}\big\|_{\Psi^{\mu}B_{p,d}^{1-2/p}(\Omega)}\simeq\big\|u_{0}\big\|_{\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}B_{p,-2\beta_{t}p}^{1-2/p}(\Omega)}\lesssim N\big\|u_{0}\big\|_{\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}H_{d/\delta,-2\beta_{t}d/\delta}^{1}(\Omega)}\simeq I\,.

Thererfore Theorem
4.12
implies that there exists a unique solution
u\in\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{1}(\Omega,T)=\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}\mathcal{H}_{p,-2-2\beta_{t}p}^{1}(\Omega,T)
of equation (
5.32
).
Moreover, by Propositions
3.17
and
4.11
(with
\beta=\beta_{t}+1/p
), we obtain

\displaystyle\big|\widetilde{\psi}^{-1+\epsilon}\big(u(t,\cdot)-u(s,\cdot)\big)\big|_{\beta_{x}}^{(0)}\lesssim\,

\displaystyle\|u(t)-u(s)\|_{\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}H_{p,0}^{d/p+\beta_{x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\,

\displaystyle|t-s|^{\beta_{t}}\|u\|_{\widetilde{\psi}^{1-\epsilon}\mathcal{H}_{p,-2-2\beta_{t}p}^{1}}

\displaystyle\simeq\,

\displaystyle|t-s|^{\beta_{t}}\|u\|_{\Psi^{\mu}\mathcal{H}_{p,d-2}^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\,

\displaystyle|t-s|^{\beta_{t}}\big(F+I\big)\,.

∎

Remark 5.19
.

Assume that
\Omega
is a bounded domain satisfying the capacity density condition (
5.8
).
By Remark
5.11
,
\Omega
satisfies
\mathbf{LHMD}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha>0
.
Let
\epsilon,\,\delta_{0}\in(0,1]
and
f^{0}
,
f^{1}
,
\ldots
,
f^{d}
be measurable functions on
\Omega
such that

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{\delta_{0}}:=\Big\|\psi^{-1+\epsilon}|\rho^{2-\delta_{0}}f^{0}|+\sum_{i}\psi^{-1+\epsilon}|\rho^{1-\delta_{0}}f^{i}|\Big\|_{L_{\infty}(\Omega)}<\infty\,.

Take small enough
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
such that (
5.26
) holds.
Then, since
\Omega
is bounded,
F
in (
5.27
) satisfies

F\lesssim_{d}\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)^{\delta_{0}}\widetilde{F}_{\delta_{0}}\,,

where
\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)
is the diameter of
\Omega
.
By Theorem
5.17
, the solution
u
of the equation

\Delta u=f^{0}+D_{i}f^{i}

satisfies

\displaystyle|u(x)|+\rho(x)^{1-\delta_{0}}\sup_{y\in B_{\rho(x)/2}(x)}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|}{|x-y|^{1-\delta_{0}}}\lesssim\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)^{\delta_{0}}F_{\delta_{0}}\cdot\big(\psi(x)\big)^{1-\epsilon}.

Remark 5.20
.

The existences of solutions in Theorem
5.17
(resp.
5.18
) follows from Theorem
3.18
(resp.
4.12
).
Therefore, the global uniqueness theorem, Theorem
3.24
(resp.
4.17
), also holds for the solutions in this corollary.

For example, suppose that
f^{0}\in L_{2,d+2}(\Omega)
and
f^{1},\,\ldots,\,f^{d}\in L_{2,d}(\Omega)
under the same assumption as in Theorem
5.17
.
Then Theorem
3.18
implies a solution
u\in H_{2,d-2}^{1}(\Omega)
of equation (
5.28
).
Furthermore, due to Theorem
5.17
and Theorem
3.24
, this
u
satisfies estimate (
5.29
).

5.3.
Domain with thin exterior : Aikawa dimension

The notion of the Aikawa dimension was first introduced by Aikawa
[
2
]
to observe the quasiadditivity of the Riesz capacity.
We recall the definition of the Aikawa dimension.
For a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the Aikawa dimension of
E
, denoted by
\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(E)
, is defined by

\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(E)=\inf\Big\{\beta\geq 0\,:\,\sup_{p\in E,\,r>0}\frac{1}{r^{\beta}}\int_{B_{r}(p)}\frac{1}{d(y,E)^{d-\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty\Big\}

with considering
\frac{1}{0}=\infty
.

In this subsection, we assume that
d\geq 3
, and
\Omega
satisfies

\beta_{0}:=\dim_{\mathcal{A}}\Omega^{c}<d-2\,.

Theorem 5.21
.

For a constant
\beta<d-2
, if there exists a constant
A_{\beta}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{p\in\Omega^{c},\,r>0}\frac{1}{r^{\beta}}\int_{B_{r}(p)}\frac{1}{d(y,\Omega^{c})^{d-\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}y\leq A_{\beta}<\infty\,,

(5.33)

then the function

\phi(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{-d+2}\rho(y)^{-d+\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

is a superharmonic function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
-\Delta\phi=N(d)\rho^{-d+\beta}
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle N^{-1}\rho(x)^{-d+2+\beta}\leq\phi(x)\leq N\rho(x)^{-d+2+\beta}\,.

(5.34)

where
N=N(d,\beta,A_{\beta})
.

Before proving Theorem
5.21
, we first look at corollaries of this theorem.

Corollary 5.22
.

The Hardy inequality (
1.3
) holds on
\Omega
, where
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega)
depends only on
d,\beta_{0}
and
\{A_{\beta}\}_{\beta>\beta_{0}}
.

Proof.

We first note that this corollary is implied by
[
2
, Theorem 3]
, which establishes that if
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\alpha>0
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy

-(p-1)\big(d-\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(\Omega^{c})\big)<\gamma<d-\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(\Omega^{c})-\alpha p\,,

then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|u(x)|^{p}}{\rho(x)^{\alpha p+\gamma}}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq N\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\big|(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x)\big|^{p}}{\rho(x)^{\gamma}}\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all}\quad u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where
N=N(d,\{A_{\beta}\}_{\beta>\dim_{\mathcal{A}}(\Omega^{c})},p,\alpha,\gamma)
, and
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big(\,|\cdot|^{\alpha}\mathcal{F}(u)\big)
.
Actually,
[
2
, Theorem 3]
is more general than this corollary, and the proof is based on Muckenhoupt’s
A_{p}
weight theory.

Considering only Corollary
5.22
, this result can be proved differently.
We first note the following inequality provided in
[
8
, Lemma 3.5.1]
: if
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
s>0
is a smooth superharmonic function on a neighborhood of
\text{supp}(f)
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{-\Delta s}{s}|f|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla f|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all}\quad f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(5.35)

(the proof of this inequality is based on integrating
\big|\nabla f-(f/s)\nabla s\big|^{2}
and performing integration by parts).
Take any
\beta\in(\beta_{0},d-2)
, and let
\phi
be the function in Theorem
5.21
, so that

\displaystyle-\Delta\phi\geq N_{1}\rho^{-2}\phi>0

(5.36)

where
N_{1}=N(d,\beta,A_{\beta})>0
.
Fix
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
.
For
0<\epsilon<d\big(\text{supp}(f),\partial\Omega\big)
, let
\phi^{(\epsilon)}
be the mollification of
\phi
in (
2.1
).
Observe that

-\Delta\big(\phi^{(\epsilon)}\big)\geq N_{1}^{-1}\big(\rho^{-2}\phi\big)^{(\epsilon)}\geq N_{1}^{-1}(\rho+\epsilon)^{-2}\phi^{(\epsilon)}\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{d}\,,

where
N_{1}
is in (
5.36
).
By appling the monotone convergence theorem to (
5.35
) with
s=\phi^{(\epsilon)}
(see Lemma
2.6
), we obtain (
1.3
) with
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Omega)=N_{1}
.
∎

Corollary 5.23
.

For any
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\beta_{0}<\theta<(d-2-\beta_{0})p+\beta_{0}\,,

Statement
5.3

(\Omega,p,\theta)
holds.
In addition,
N_{1}
in (
5.2
) and
N_{2}
in (
5.3
) depend only on
d
,
p
,
\gamma
,
\theta
,
\beta_{0}
,
\{\mathcal{A}_{\beta}\}_{\beta>\beta_{0}}
.

Proof of Corollary
5.23
.

Choose
\beta\in(\beta_{0},d-2)
satisfying

\beta<\theta<(d-2-\beta)p+\beta\,.

By Theorem
5.21
, there exists a superharmonic function
\phi
satisfying
\phi\sim\rho^{-d+2+\beta}
, and therefore
by Remark
5.4
, the proof is completed.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.21
.

We first prove (
5.34
).
For a fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exists
p_{x}\in\partial\Omega
such that
|x-p_{x}|=\rho(x)=:\rho_{x}
.
Put

E_{0}=B(x,2^{-1}\rho_{x})\quad\text{and}\quad E_{j}=B(x,2^{j-1}\rho_{x})\setminus B(x,2^{j-2}\rho_{x})

for
j=1,\,2,\,\ldots
, and put

I_{j}=\int_{E_{j}}|x-y|^{-d+2}\rho(y)^{-d+\beta}dy

for
j=0,\,1,\,2,\,\ldots
, so that
\phi(x)=\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}I_{j}
.
If
y\in E_{0}
then
\frac{1}{2}\rho_{x}\leq\rho(y)\leq 2\rho_{x}
, which implies

I_{0}\simeq_{d,\beta}\rho_{x}^{-d+\beta}\int_{B(x,\rho_{x}/2)}|x-y|^{-d+2}\,\mathrm{d}y\simeq_{d}\rho_{x}^{-d+2+\beta}\,.

For
I_{j}
,
j\geq 1
, observe that

\displaystyle 0\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}I_{j}\,

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(2^{j-2}\rho_{x})^{-d+2}\int_{B(x,2^{j-1}\rho_{x})}\rho(y)^{-d+\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j(d-2)}\rho_{x}^{-d+2}\int_{B(p_{x},2^{j}\rho_{x})}\rho(y)^{-d+\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq N\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j(d-2-\beta)}\Big)\rho_{x}^{-d+2+\beta}\,.

where
N=N(d,\beta,A_{\beta})
.
Since the summation in the last term is finit, (
5.34
) is proved.

To prove that
-\Delta\phi=N(d)\phi
in the sense of distribution, recall that

-\Delta_{x}\Big(|x-y|^{-d+2}\Big)=N(d)\,\delta_{0}(x-y)

in the sense of distribution, where
\delta_{0}(\cdot)
is the Dirac delta distribution.
Due to (
5.33
) and
\phi\simeq\rho^{-d+2+\beta}
,
\phi
is locally integrable.
Therefore, by the Fubini theorem, for any
\zeta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\big(-\Delta\zeta)(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{-d+2}(-\Delta\zeta)(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)\rho(y)^{-d+\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=N(d)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\zeta(y)\rho(y)^{-d+\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y\,.

∎

6.
Application II - Various domains with fat exterior

In this section, we present results for the exterior cone condition, convex domains, the exterior Reifenberg condition, and Lipschitz cones.
These domains and conditions imply the fat exterior condition.

Throughout this section, we consider a domain
\Omega\subsetneq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\geq 2
.

6.1.
Exterior cone condition and exterior line segment condition

Definition 6.1

(Exterior cone condition)
.

For
\delta\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2})
and
R\in(0,\infty]
, a domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to satisfy the
exterior
(\delta,R)
-cone condition
if for every
p\in\partial\Omega
, there exists a unit vector
e_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\{x\in B_{R}(p)\,:\,(x-p)\cdot e_{p}\geq|x-p|\cos\delta\}\subset\Omega^{c}\,.

(6.1)

Note that the left hand side of (
6.1
) is obtained by applying a translation and a rotation to the set

\{x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in B_{R}(0)\,:\,x_{1}\geq|x|\cos\delta\}\,.

The exterior
(0,R)
-cone condition can be called the
exterior
R
-line segment condition
, due to

\{x\in B_{R}(p)\,:\,(x-p)\cdot e_{p}\geq|x-p|\}=\{p+re_{p}\,:\,r\in[0,R)\}\,.

For examples of the exterior cone condition and exterior line segment condition, see Figure
6.1
below.

A. Lipschitz boundary
condition
B. Exterior
(\frac{\pi}{3},\infty)
-cone condition
(does not satisfy Lipschitz
boundary conddition)
C. Exterior
\infty
-line
segment condition
(does not satisfy
(\delta,R)
-cone
condition,
\forall\,\,\,\delta,\,R>0
)

Figure 6.1.
Examples for exterior cone condition

Example 6.2
.

Suppose that there exists
K,\,R\in(0,\infty]
such that for any
p\in\partial\Omega
, there exists a function
f_{p}\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
such that

\displaystyle|f_{p}(y^{\prime})-f_{p}(z^{\prime})|\leq K|y^{\prime}-z^{\prime}|\quad\text{for all}\quad y^{\prime},\,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\,\,\,,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\qquad\,\,\,

(6.2)

\displaystyle\Omega\cap B_{R}(p)=\big\{y=(y^{\prime},y_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}\,:\,y_{d}>f_{p}(y^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad|y|<R\big\}

(6.3)

where
(y^{\prime},y_{d})=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{d})
in (
6.3
) is an orthonormal coordinate system centered at
p
.
Then
\Omega
satisfies the exterior
(\delta,R)
-cone condition, where
\delta=\textmd{arctan}(1/K)\in[0,\pi/2)
.

Moreover, if
f\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
satisfies (
6.2
) with
f_{p}=f
, then a domain

\big\{(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}\,:\,x_{n}>f(x^{\prime})\big\}

satisfies the exterior
(\delta,\infty)
-cone condition, where
\delta=\arctan(1/K)
.

For
\delta\in(0,\pi)
,
let

E_{\delta}:=\{\sigma\in\partial B_{1}(0)\,:\,\sigma_{1}>-\cos\delta\}

(see Figure
6.2
below).

\delta

Figure 6.2.
E_{\delta}

We denote the first eigenvalue of the Dirichlet spherical Laplacian on
E_{\delta}
as
\Lambda_{\delta}
(see Proposition
6.24
.(1)).
Alternatively, we can express
\Lambda_{\delta}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\delta}=\inf_{f\in F_{\pi-\delta}}\frac{\int_{0}^{\pi-\delta}|f^{\prime}(\theta)|^{2}(\sin\theta)^{d-2}d\theta}{\int_{0}^{\pi-\delta}|f(\theta)|^{2}(\sin\theta)^{d-2}d\theta}\,,

(6.4)

where
F_{\pi-\delta}
is the set of all non-zero Lipschitz continuous function
f:[0,\pi-\delta]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
f(\pi-\delta)=0
(see
[
19
]
).

We also define

\lambda_{\delta}:=-\frac{d-2}{2}+\sqrt{\Big(\frac{d-2}{2}\Big)^{2}+\Lambda_{\delta}}\,,

and when
d=2
, we define
\lambda_{0}=\frac{1}{2}
.

The following quantitative information of
\Lambda_{\delta}
and
\lambda_{\delta}
is provided in
[
9
]
:

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\delta\in(0,\pi)
.

(1)

If
d=2
then
\lambda_{\delta}=\sqrt{\Lambda_{\delta}}=\frac{\pi}{2(\pi-\delta)}>\frac{1}{2}
.

(2)

If
d=4
then
\lambda_{\delta}=-1+\sqrt{1+\Lambda_{\delta}}=\frac{\delta}{\pi-\delta}
.

(3)

For
d\geq 3
,

\Lambda_{\delta}\geq\left(\int_{0}^{\pi-\delta}(\sin t)^{-d+2}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}(\sin r)^{d-2}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)\,\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{-1}.

Moreover,
\Lambda_{\pi/2}=d-1
,
\lim\limits_{\delta\searrow 0}\Lambda_{\delta}=0
, and
\lim\limits_{\delta\nearrow\pi}\Lambda_{\delta}=+\infty
.

Note that when
d=3
,
\Lambda_{\delta}\geq\frac{1}{2}|\log\,\sin\frac{\delta}{2}|^{-1}
.

Remark 6.4
.

For each
\delta>0
, there is a function
F\in C\big(\overline{E_{\delta}}\big)\cap C^{\infty}(E_{\delta})
such that

F>0\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}F+\Lambda_{\delta}F=0\quad\text{on}\,\,\,\,E_{\delta}\,\,\,,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,F|_{\overline{E_{\delta}}\setminus E_{\delta}}\equiv 0

(see,
e.g.
,
[
19
, Section 5]
), where
C^{\infty}(E_{\delta})
and
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}
are introduced in Subsection
6.4
.
It follows from (
6.41
) that the function

v_{\delta}(x):=|x|^{\lambda_{\delta}}F(x/|x|)

is harmonic on

U_{\delta}:=\big\{y\in B_{1}(0)\,:\,y_{1}>-|y|\cos\delta\big\}\,,

and vanishes on
\partial U_{\delta}\cap B_{1}(0)
.

With help of
\lambda_{\delta}
, we state main results of this subsection.

Theorem 6.5
.

For

\displaystyle\delta\in[0,\pi/2)\quad\text{if}\,\,\,\,d=2\,\,,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\quad\delta\in(0,\pi/2)\quad\text{if}\,\,\,\,d\geq 3\,,

let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfy the exterior
(\delta,R)
-cone condition, where

R\in(0,\infty]\quad\text{if}\,\,\,\,\text{$\Omega$\, is\, bounded}\,\,,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\quad R=\infty\quad\text{if}\,\,\,\,\text{$\Omega$\, is\, unbounded.}

Then
\Omega
satisfies
\mathbf{LHMD}(\lambda_{\delta})
, where
M_{\lambda_{\delta}}
in (
5.6
) depends only on
d
,
\delta
and
\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)/R
.
If
\Omega
is unbounded (and
R=\infty
), then for we can drop the dependence of
M_{\lambda_{\delta}}
on
\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)/R
.

Before the proof of Theorem
6.5
, we state a corollary which follows from Theorem
6.5
and Corollary
5.13
.

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
.
Under the same assumption of Theorem
6.5
, if
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfies

-2-(p-1)\lambda_{\delta}<\theta-d<-2+\lambda_{\delta}\,,

then Statement
5.3

(\Omega,p,\theta)
holds.
In addition,
N_{1}
in (
5.2
) and
N_{2}
in (
5.3
) depend only on
d,\,p,\,\theta,\,\gamma,\,\delta,\,\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)/R
, and if
\Omega
is unbounded (and
R=\infty
) then we can drop the dependence of
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
on
\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)/R
.

To prove Theorem
6.5
we use the boundary Harnack principle on Lipschitz domains.

Proposition 6.7

(see Theorem 1 of
[
75
]
)
.

Let
D
be a bounded Lipschitz domain,
A
be a relatively open subset of
\partial D
, and
U
be a subdomain of
D
with
\partial U\cap\partial D\subset A
(see Figure
6.3
below).
Then there exists
N=N(D,A,U)>0
such that if
u,v
are positive harmonic funtion on
D
, and vanish on
E
, then

\frac{u(x)}{v(x)}\leq N\frac{u(x_{0})}{v(x_{0})}\quad\text{for any}\quad x_{0},\,x\in U\,.

D
U
A

Figure 6.3.
D
,
A
, and
U
in Proposition
6.7

The boundary Harnack principle has also been established for a more general class of domains, so-called non-tangentially accessible domains, by Jerison and Kenig
[
29
]
.

Proof of Theorem
6.5
.

By Remark
5.10
, it is sufficient to prove that there exists a constant
M>0
such that

\displaystyle w(x,p,r)\leq M\Big(\frac{|x-p|}{r}\Big)^{\lambda_{\delta}}\quad\text{for all}\,\,\,x\in\Omega\cap B(p,r)

whenever
p\in\partial\Omega
and
r\in(0,R)
.
For any
p\in\partial\Omega
, there exists a unit vector
e_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

C_{p}:=\{y\in B_{R}(p)\,:\,(y-p)\cdot e_{p}\geq|y-p|\cos\delta\}\subset\Omega^{c}\,.

Since

\Omega\cap B_{r}(p)\subset B_{r}(p)\setminus C_{p}\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega\cap\partial B_{r}(p)\subset\partial B_{r}(p)\setminus C_{p}\,,

we have

\displaystyle w(x,p,r)\leq w\big(x,\,B_{r}(p)\setminus C_{p}\,,\,\partial B_{r}(p)\setminus C_{p}\,\big),

(6.5)

by directly applying the definition of
w(\cdot,p,r)
(see (
5.5
)).
Consider a rotation map
T
such that
T(e_{p})=(-1,0,\ldots,0)
, and put
T_{0}(x)=r^{-1}T(x-p)
.
Then

\displaystyle w\big(x,\,B_{r}(p)\setminus C_{p}\,,\,\partial B_{r}(p)\setminus C_{p}\,\big)=w\big(T_{0}(x),U_{\delta},E_{\delta}\big),

(6.6)

where

U_{\delta}=\{y\in B_{1}(0)\,:\,y_{1}>-|y|\cos\delta\}\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,E_{\delta}=\{y\in\partial B_{1}(0)\,:\,y_{1}>-|y|\cos\delta\}\,.

Due to (
6.5
) and (
6.6
), it is sufficient to show that there exists a constant
M>0
depending only on
d
and
\delta
such that

\displaystyle w(x,U_{\delta},E_{\delta})\leq M|x|^{\lambda_{\delta}}\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in U_{\delta}\,,

(6.7)

-
Case 1.

\delta>0
.

Put
v(x)=|x|^{\lambda_{\delta}}F_{0}(x/|x|)

where
F_{0}
is the first Dirichlet eigenfunction of spherical laplacian on
E_{\delta}\subset\partial B_{1}(0)
, with
\sup_{E_{\delta}}F_{0}=1
(see Remark
6.4
).
Note that
U_{\delta}
is a bounded Lipschitz domain, and
w(\,\cdot\,,U_{\delta},E_{\delta})
and
v
are positive harmonic functions on
U_{\delta}
, and vanish on
\partial U_{\delta}\cap B_{1}
.
By applying Proposition
6.7
for
D=U_{\delta}
,
A=(\partial U_{\delta})\cap B_{1}(0)
, and
U=U_{\delta}\cap B_{1/2}(0)
, we obtain that there exists a constant
N_{0}=N_{0}(d,\delta)>0
such that

w(x,U_{\delta},E_{\delta})\leq N_{0}v(x)\leq N_{0}|x|^{\lambda_{\delta}}\quad\text{for}\quad x\in U_{\delta}\cap B_{1/2}(0).

Therefore (
6.7
) is obtained, where
M_{0}=N_{0}\vee 2^{\lambda_{0}}
.

-
Case 2.

\delta=0
and
d=2
.

We consider
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as
\mathbb{C}
.
Note

U_{0}=\{re^{i\theta}\,:\,r\in(0,1),\,\theta\in(-\pi,\pi)\}\,\,,\,\,\,\,E_{0}=\{e^{i\theta}\,:\,\theta\in(-\pi,\pi)\}\,.

Observe that a function
s
is a classical superharmonic function on
U_{0}
if and only if
s(z^{2})
is a classical superharmonic function on
B_{1}(0)\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
(use Proposition
2.4
).
It is implied by the definition of PWB solutions (see (
5.5
)) that

w(z^{2},U_{0},E_{0})=w\big(z,B_{1}(0)\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2},\partial B_{1}(0)\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\big)\,.

Since the map
z=(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto z_{1}
is harmonic on
B_{1}(0)\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, by Proposition
6.7
with
D=B_{1}(0)\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle w\big(z,B_{1}(0)\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2},\big(\partial B_{1}(0)\big)\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\big)\leq N|z|\quad\text{for}\,\,\,z\in B_{1/2}(0)\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\,,

(6.8)

where
N
depends on nothing.
Therefore the proof is completed.
∎

6.2.
Convex domains

We recall the definition of convex set.
A set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to be
convex
if
(1-t)x+ty\in E
for any
x,\,y\in E
and
t\in[0,1]
.

Remark 6.8
.

We claim that for an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\Omega
is convex if and only if for any
p\in\partial\Omega
, there exists a unit vector
e_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\Omega\subset\{x\,:\,(x-p)\cdot e_{p}<0\}=:U_{p}

(6.9)

(see Figure
6.4
below).

e_{p}
F_{p}

Figure 6.4.
e_{p}
in (
6.9
), and
F_{p}
in (
6.13
)

Let
\Omega
be a convex domain, and fix
p\in\partial\Omega
.
Since the set
\{p\}
is convex and disjoint from
\Omega
, the hyperplane separation theorem (see,
e.g.
,
[
65
, Theorem 3.4.(a)]
) implies that there exists a unit vector
e_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that (
6.9
) holds.

Conversely, suppose that for every
p\in\partial\Omega
, there exist a unit vector
e_{p}
satisfying (
6.9
).
Then
E:=\bigcap_{p\in\partial\Omega}U_{p}
is convex,
\Omega\subset E
, and
E\cap\partial\Omega=\emptyset
. These imply
E=\Omega
. Therefore our claim is proved.

Remark 6.9
.

The argument to obtain (
6.8
) also implies that for any
d\in\mathbb{N}
,

w\big(x,B_{1}(0)\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},(\partial B_{1}(0))\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\big)\leq N(d)|x|\quad\text{for all}\,\,\,x\in B_{1}(0)\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\,.

By translation, dilation and rotation, we obtain that for a convex domain
\Omega
and
p\in\partial\Omega
,

w(x,p,r)\leq w(x,B_{r}(p)\cap U_{p},\big(\partial B_{r}(p)\big)\cap U_{p})\leq N(d)\frac{|x-p|}{r}

for all
x\in B_{r}(p)\cap\Omega
, where
U_{p}
